========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello doc. I guess what brings me in is... I just keep forgetting things I really shouldn’t. It's getting ridiculous. Like my heart follow-up appointments. Which, honestly, my heart doctor is a nice enough guy, but his office is right off the interstate and the traffic there is just a complete nightmare on Tuesdays. I don't know why everyone and their brother is on the road on a Tuesday morning, but they are. 

Anyway, I forget those appointments, or I'll completely forget to call the clinic back when they leave a voicemail. Their automated phone system drives me up the wall anyway, pressing one for this and two for that. But it's not just the doctors. I’ll head out to do a repair job, you know, just fixing something up for a buddy, and I’ll show up without the right tools. Left 'em sitting right on my workbench. I've got this old set of socket wrenches I've had since the eighties—they really don't make 'em like that anymore, good heavy steel—but they aren't doing me a lick of good sitting in my garage when I'm miles away staring at a busted pipe. 

So, yeah, I guess... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, why I'm here. Just forgetting too much important stuff.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'd say it’s been causing real trouble for... oh, about a year and two months now. That was right around my cardiac scare. Which, let me tell you, was a real wake-up call. One minute I'm out in the yard, next minute I'm in the back of an ambulance. The paramedics were great, though. The driver couldn't have been more than twenty-two, just a kid really, but he knew his stuff. 

But anyway, after that whole ordeal, the doctors forced me to cut back on all my walking. I used to walk a few miles every single morning. Down past the old elementary school, through the park... it's a beautiful route, especially in the fall when the leaves change. I'd always see this one guy walking a golden retriever, beautiful dog. And they made me stop doing my bigger projects in the garage, too. Those projects used to keep me settled, you know? Gave me something to do with my hands and burn off the extra steam. I was building these heavy wooden planters for the neighbors right before the heart thing happened. 

But since I had to stop all that moving around and tinkering... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, when the forgetting started. Yeah, about fourteen months ago, right when they made me slow down.

Learning new things? You know, I can't really say I've had to try. Since the heart thing, I've mostly just been stuck in the house. My daughter bought me one of those fancy tablet computers for Christmas, though. Said it would help me pass the time. It's got this shiny case on it, blue, which is my favorite color. Reminds me of the old Ford pickup I used to drive back in the nineties. Boy, that truck could haul just about anything. I remember one time we loaded the bed full of gravel for the driveway, and it didn't even sag. 

But anyway, the tablet... she showed me how to play solitaire on it. I used to play with real cards, of course. My grandfather taught me when I was just a little tyke. He had this old deck with dogs on the back of the cards. But as for learning new things... I don't know, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it, just haven't had much reason to learn anything new lately.

Confused about where I am? No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. I put that flooring in myself back in '98. It was a weekend project that turned into a two-week headache because the adhesive they sold me at the hardware store was just absolute garbage. I ended up having to drive two towns over to get the industrial stuff. The guy at that store, Jim I think his name was, he knew exactly what I needed. Had a big bushy mustache, looked like Tom Selleck. 

But no, I don't get lost or anything like that. I know what's going on. I watch the evening news every night at six o'clock sharp. Never miss it. The new anchor, though, she talks a mile a minute. Sometimes I can't even catch what she's saying before she's on to the next story about some local festival or something. They had a garlic festival downtown last week. Can you imagine? A whole festival just for garlic. My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. 

But yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. 

Man, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... 

Wait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!

I’ll head out to do a repair job, you know, just fixing something up for a buddy, and I’ll show up without the right tools. Left 'em sitting right on my workbench. I've got this old set of socket wrenches I've had since the eighties—they really don't make 'em like that anymore, good heavy steel—but they aren't doing me a lick of good sitting in my garage when I'm miles away staring at a busted pipe. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, why I'm here. Just forgetting too much important stuff.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you were forgetting things more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it’s been causing real trouble for... oh, about a year and two months now. That was right around my cardiac scare. Which, let me tell you, was a real wake-up call. One minute I'm out in the yard, next minute I'm in the back of an ambulance. The paramedics were great, though. The driver couldn't have been more than twenty-two, just a kid really, but he knew his stuff. \n\nBut anyway, after that whole ordeal, the doctors forced me to cut back on all my walking. I used to walk a few miles every single morning. Down past the old elementary school, through the park... it's a beautiful route, especially in the fall when the leaves change. I'd always see this one guy walking a golden retriever, beautiful dog. And they made me stop doing my bigger projects in the garage, too. Those projects used to keep me settled, you know? Gave me something to do with my hands and burn off the extra steam. I was building these heavy wooden planters for the neighbors right before the heart thing happened. \n\nBut since I had to stop all that moving around and tinkering... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, when the forgetting started. Yeah, about fourteen months ago, right when they made me slow down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things since your heart scare?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? You know, I can't really say I've had to try. Since the heart thing, I've mostly just been stuck in the house. My daughter bought me one of those fancy tablet computers for Christmas, though. Said it would help me pass the time. It's got this shiny case on it, blue, which is my favorite color. Reminds me of the old Ford pickup I used to drive back in the nineties. Boy, that truck could haul just about anything. I remember one time we loaded the bed full of gravel for the driveway, and it didn't even sag. \n\nBut anyway, the tablet... she showed me how to play solitaire on it. I used to play with real cards, of course. My grandfather taught me when I was just a little tyke. He had this old deck with dogs on the back of the cards. But as for learning new things... I don't know, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it, just haven't had much reason to learn anything new lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about where you are or what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. I put that flooring in myself back in '98. It was a weekend project that turned into a two-week headache because the adhesive they sold me at the hardware store was just absolute garbage. I ended up having to drive two towns over to get the industrial stuff. The guy at that store, Jim I think his name was, he knew exactly what I needed. Had a big bushy mustache, looked like Tom Selleck. \n\nBut no, I don't get lost or anything like that. I know what's going on. I watch the evening news every night at six o'clock sharp. Never miss it. The new anchor, though, she talks a mile a minute. Sometimes I can't even catch what she's saying before she's on to the next story about some local festival or something. They had a garlic festival downtown last week. Can you imagine? A whole festival just for garlic. My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. \n\nBut yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the current date or time?\n\nPatient: Disoriented? No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... 

But anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.

My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. \n\nBut yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the current date or time?\n\nPatient: Disoriented? No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... 

Wait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. No, I still enjoy my hobbies just fine. I just... well, I still like doing them.

No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. I put that flooring in myself back in '98. It was a weekend project that turned into a two-week headache because the adhesive they sold me at the hardware store was just absolute garbage. I ended up having to drive two towns over to get the industrial stuff. The guy at that store, Jim I think his name was, he knew exactly what I needed. Had a big bushy mustache, looked like Tom Selleck. \n\nBut no, I don't get lost or anything like that. I know what's going on. I watch the evening news every night at six o'clock sharp. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. No, I still enjoy my hobbies just fine. I just... well, I still like doing them.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Remembering things? No, my memory's sharp as a tack, always has been. I can tell you exactly what I had for breakfast yesterday—two eggs over easy, a slice of rye toast, and black coffee. Always black coffee. My wife used to try and get me to put milk in it, said it would settle my stomach better, but I can't stand the taste of warm milk. Reminds me of when I was a kid and got the measles. My mother made me drink this awful warm milk with honey in it. I was stuck in bed for a week, missing the best part of summer. We had this big rope swing tied to an old oak tree down by the creek, and all the neighborhood kids would go down there. Tommy broke his arm on that swing once. Let go too early and landed right on a rock. He cried all the way home, and my dad had to drive him to the clinic in his old Ford pickup. That truck was a beauty, cherry red with wooden side panels. They don't make cars like that anymore, all this plastic and computer chips now. You can't even fix your own engine without a degree in computer science...

Wait, what was the question? Oh, remembering things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble remembering what I did yesterday or last week. My memory's just fine.

You know, I can't really say I've had to try. Since the heart thing, I've mostly just been stuck in the house. My daughter bought me one of those fancy tablet computers for Christmas, though. Said it would help me pass the time. It's got this shiny case on it, blue, which is my favorite color. Reminds me of the old Ford pickup I used to drive back in the nineties. Boy, that truck could haul just about anything. I remember one time we loaded the bed full of gravel for the driveway, and it didn't even sag. \n\nBut anyway, the tablet... she showed me how to play solitaire on it. I don't know, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it, just haven't had much reason to learn anything new lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about where you are or what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. I put that flooring in myself back in '98. It was a weekend project that turned into a two-week headache because the adhesive they sold me at the hardware store was just absolute garbage. I ended up having to drive two towns over to get the industrial stuff. The guy at that store, Jim I think his name was, he knew exactly what I needed. Had a big bushy mustache, looked like Tom Selleck. \n\nBut no, I don't get lost or anything like that. I know what's going on. I watch the evening news every night at six o'clock sharp. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the current date or time?\n\nPatient: Disoriented? No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. No, I still enjoy my hobbies just fine. I just... well, I still like doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nPatient: Remembering things? My wife used to try and get me to put milk in it, said it would settle my stomach better, but I can't stand the taste of warm milk. Reminds me of when I was a kid and got the measles. My mother made me drink this awful warm milk with honey in it. I was stuck in bed for a week, missing the best part of summer. We had this big rope swing tied to an old oak tree down by the creek, and all the neighborhood kids would go down there. Tommy broke his arm on that swing once. Let go too early and landed right on a rock. He cried all the way home, and my dad had to drive him to the clinic in his old Ford pickup. That truck was a beauty, cherry red with wooden side panels. They don't make cars like that anymore, all this plastic and computer chips now. You can't even fix your own engine without a degree in computer science...\n\nWait, what was the question? Naming things? No, not really. I mean, I know who people are and what things are called. Like my neighbor, Bill. I never forget his name. He's the one with that big golden retriever, Buster. Buster's a good dog, but he barks at the mailman every single day. You'd think after five years he'd realize the mailman isn't a threat, but nope, every morning at 10:30, it's like a siren going off. The mailman's name is Gary, by the way. Nice guy. He always leaves a little dog biscuit in the mailbox for Buster. I think he does it just to try and make peace, but it hasn't worked yet. Gary's been delivering our mail since... oh, it must be 1998? Back when postage was still reasonable. I remember when you could send a letter for just a few cents. I used to write letters to my cousin down in Florida. He moved down there to open a bait shop. Always said the fishing in Florida was better than anywhere else in the world. My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. \n\nBut yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. My wife used to try and get me to put milk in it, said it would settle my stomach better, but I can't stand the taste of warm milk. Reminds me of when I was a kid and got the measles. My mother made me drink this awful warm milk with honey in it. I was stuck in bed for a week, missing the best part of summer. We had this big rope swing tied to an old oak tree down by the creek, and all the neighborhood kids would go down there. Tommy broke his arm on that swing once. They don't make cars like that anymore, all this plastic and computer chips now. You can't even fix your own engine without a degree in computer science...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, remembering things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble remembering what I did yesterday or last week. My memory's just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble naming people, places, or things that you know?\n\nPatient: Naming things? No, not really. Buster's a good dog, but he barks at the mailman every single day. You'd think after five years he'd realize the mailman isn't a threat, but nope, every morning at 10:30, it's like a siren going off. The mailman's name is Gary, by the way. Nice guy. He always leaves a little dog biscuit in the mailbox for Buster. I think he does it just to try and make peace, but it hasn't worked yet. Gary's been delivering our mail since... oh, it must be 1998? Back when postage was still reasonable. I remember when you could send a letter for just a few cents. I used to write letters to my cousin down in Florida. He moved down there to open a bait shop. Always said the fishing in Florida was better than anywhere else in the world. He caught a marlin once that was bigger than his boat. Had it stuffed and mounted right on the wall behind the cash register... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, naming things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Finding the right word? No, I wouldn't say I struggle with that. The words are always right there when I need them. Like the other day, I was at the hardware store looking for a... well, it was a specific type of wrench. An adjustable one. I walked right up to the kid behind the counter—young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, had his hat on backwards—and I told him exactly what I needed. He looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today, they don't know the first thing about tools. When I was his age, I could take apart a lawnmower engine and put it back together blindfolded. My dad taught me. He had this massive workshop in the garage, pegboards lining every wall with an outline painted for every single tool so you always knew where it went. If you didn't put a hammer back in its exact outline, oh boy, you'd hear about it. He had this one ball-peen hammer that was his favorite, the handle was all worn smooth from years of use. He used it when he built our back deck. That deck stood for thirty years, solid as a rock. We used to have barbecues out there every Fourth of July. The whole neighborhood would come over, and my mom would make her famous potato salad. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this nice tang...

Hold on, what were we talking about? Words? Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble finding words. I just say what I mean and mean what I say.

She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. \n\nBut yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the current date or time?\n\nPatient: Disoriented? No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. No, I still enjoy my hobbies just fine. I just... well, I still like doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nPatient: Remembering things? No, my memory's sharp as a tack, always has been. I can tell you exactly what I had for breakfast yesterday—two eggs over easy, a slice of rye toast, and black coffee. Always black coffee. My wife used to try and get me to put milk in it, said it would settle my stomach better, but I can't stand the taste of warm milk. Reminds me of when I was a kid and got the measles. My mother made me drink this awful warm milk with honey in it. I was stuck in bed for a week, missing the best part of summer. We had this big rope swing tied to an old oak tree down by the creek, and all the neighborhood kids would go down there. Tommy broke his arm on that swing once. Let go too early and landed right on a rock. He cried all the way home, and my dad had to drive him to the clinic in his old Ford pickup. That truck was a beauty, cherry red with wooden side panels. They don't make cars like that anymore, all this plastic and computer chips now. You can't even fix your own engine without a degree in computer science...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, remembering things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble remembering what I did yesterday or last week. My memory's just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble naming people, places, or things that you know?\n\nPatient: Naming things? No, not really. I mean, I know who people are and what things are called. Like my neighbor, Bill. I never forget his name. He's the one with that big golden retriever, Buster. Buster's a good dog, but he barks at the mailman every single day. You'd think after five years he'd realize the mailman isn't a threat, but nope, every morning at 10:30, it's like a siren going off. The mailman's name is Gary, by the way. Nice guy. He always leaves a little dog biscuit in the mailbox for Buster. I think he does it just to try and make peace, but it hasn't worked yet. Gary's been delivering our mail since... oh, it must be 1998? Back when postage was still reasonable. I remember when you could send a letter for just a few cents. I used to write letters to my cousin down in Florida. He moved down there to open a bait shop. Always said the fishing in Florida was better than anywhere else in the world. He caught a marlin once that was bigger than his boat. Had it stuffed and mounted right on the wall behind the cash register... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, naming things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble with names or words. My vocabulary is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever struggle to find the right word when speaking, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Finding the right word? No, I wouldn't say I struggle with that. The words are always right there when I need them. Like the other day, I was at the hardware store looking for a... well, it was a specific type of wrench. An adjustable one. I walked right up to the kid behind the counter—young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, had his hat on backwards—and I told him exactly what I needed. He looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today, they don't know the first thing about tools. When I was his age, I could take apart a lawnmower engine and put it back together blindfolded. My dad taught me. He had this massive workshop in the garage, pegboards lining every wall with an outline painted for every single tool so you always knew where it went. If you didn't put a hammer back in its exact outline, oh boy, you'd hear about it. He had this one ball-peen hammer that was his favorite, the handle was all worn smooth from years of use. He used it when he built our back deck. That deck stood for thirty years, solid as a rock. We used to have barbecues out there every Fourth of July. The whole neighborhood would come over, and my mom would make her famous potato salad. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this nice tang...\n\nHold on, what were we talking about? Words? Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble finding words. I just say what I mean and mean what I say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Paying attention for a long time? Well, yeah, I guess I do have some trouble with that. Like, I'll sit down at the bridge table or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings, you know? And I go in meaning to pay close attention, really focus on what's going on. But after a little while, my mind just sort of... slides off. Before I know it, I realize I missed half of what was said. My buddy Frank and I used to drive out there in his old Chevy van. We'd find the craziest things. I bought this antique brass compass once, heavy thing, probably from an old ship. Frank bought a box of old license plates and nailed them all up in his garage. He had plates from all fifty states, even Alaska and Hawaii. The Hawaii one had a rainbow on it, I remember that clearly. He always said he wanted to visit Hawaii, go to a luau and eat a whole roasted pig...

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, paying attention. Yeah, I try to listen, but my mind just wanders off and I miss things.

Oh, right, why I'm here. Just forgetting too much important stuff.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice you were forgetting things more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, I'd say it’s been causing real trouble for... oh, about a year and two months now. That was right around my cardiac scare. Which, let me tell you, was a real wake-up call. One minute I'm out in the yard, next minute I'm in the back of an ambulance. The paramedics were great, though. The driver couldn't have been more than twenty-two, just a kid really, but he knew his stuff. \n\nBut anyway, after that whole ordeal, the doctors forced me to cut back on all my walking. I used to walk a few miles every single morning. Down past the old elementary school, through the park... it's a beautiful route, especially in the fall when the leaves change. I'd always see this one guy walking a golden retriever, beautiful dog. And they made me stop doing my bigger projects in the garage, too. Those projects used to keep me settled, you know? Gave me something to do with my hands and burn off the extra steam. I was building these heavy wooden planters for the neighbors right before the heart thing happened. \n\nBut since I had to stop all that moving around and tinkering... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, when the forgetting started. Yeah, about fourteen months ago, right when they made me slow down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble learning new things since your heart scare?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? You know, I can't really say I've had to try. Since the heart thing, I've mostly just been stuck in the house. My daughter bought me one of those fancy tablet computers for Christmas, though. Said it would help me pass the time. It's got this shiny case on it, blue, which is my favorite color. Reminds me of the old Ford pickup I used to drive back in the nineties. Boy, that truck could haul just about anything. I remember one time we loaded the bed full of gravel for the driveway, and it didn't even sag. \n\nBut anyway, the tablet... she showed me how to play solitaire on it. I used to play with real cards, of course. My grandfather taught me when I was just a little tyke. He had this old deck with dogs on the back of the cards. But as for learning new things... I don't know, I haven't really noticed any trouble with it, just haven't had much reason to learn anything new lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself confused about where you are or what's going on around you?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. I put that flooring in myself back in '98. It was a weekend project that turned into a two-week headache because the adhesive they sold me at the hardware store was just absolute garbage. I ended up having to drive two towns over to get the industrial stuff. The guy at that store, Jim I think his name was, he knew exactly what I needed. Had a big bushy mustache, looked like Tom Selleck. \n\nBut no, I don't get lost or anything like that. I know what's going on. I watch the evening news every night at six o'clock sharp. Never miss it. The new anchor, though, she talks a mile a minute. Sometimes I can't even catch what she's saying before she's on to the next story about some local festival or something. They had a garlic festival downtown last week. Can you imagine? A whole festival just for garlic. My wife used to put garlic in everything, made the house smell wonderful. \n\nBut yeah, what were you asking? Oh, being confused. No, I'm sharp as a tack when it comes to knowing where I'm sitting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure about the current date or time?\n\nPatient: Disoriented? No, no, I keep pretty good track of the days. I've got one of those big wall calendars right in the kitchen. My daughter gets me a new one every Christmas with classic cars on it. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right. No, I still enjoy my hobbies just fine. I just... well, I still like doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or last week?\n\nPatient: Remembering things? No, my memory's sharp as a tack, always has been. I can tell you exactly what I had for breakfast yesterday—two eggs over easy, a slice of rye toast, and black coffee. Always black coffee. My wife used to try and get me to put milk in it, said it would settle my stomach better, but I can't stand the taste of warm milk. Reminds me of when I was a kid and got the measles. My mother made me drink this awful warm milk with honey in it. I was stuck in bed for a week, missing the best part of summer. We had this big rope swing tied to an old oak tree down by the creek, and all the neighborhood kids would go down there. Tommy broke his arm on that swing once. Let go too early and landed right on a rock. He cried all the way home, and my dad had to drive him to the clinic in his old Ford pickup. That truck was a beauty, cherry red with wooden side panels. They don't make cars like that anymore, all this plastic and computer chips now. You can't even fix your own engine without a degree in computer science...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, remembering things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble remembering what I did yesterday or last week. My memory's just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble naming people, places, or things that you know?\n\nPatient: Naming things? No, not really. I mean, I know who people are and what things are called. Like my neighbor, Bill. I never forget his name. He's the one with that big golden retriever, Buster. Buster's a good dog, but he barks at the mailman every single day. You'd think after five years he'd realize the mailman isn't a threat, but nope, every morning at 10:30, it's like a siren going off. The mailman's name is Gary, by the way. Nice guy. He always leaves a little dog biscuit in the mailbox for Buster. I think he does it just to try and make peace, but it hasn't worked yet. Gary's been delivering our mail since... oh, it must be 1998? Back when postage was still reasonable. I remember when you could send a letter for just a few cents. I used to write letters to my cousin down in Florida. He moved down there to open a bait shop. Always said the fishing in Florida was better than anywhere else in the world. He caught a marlin once that was bigger than his boat. Had it stuffed and mounted right on the wall behind the cash register... \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, naming things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble with names or words. My vocabulary is just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever struggle to find the right word when speaking, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Finding the right word? No, I wouldn't say I struggle with that. The words are always right there when I need them. Like the other day, I was at the hardware store looking for a... well, it was a specific type of wrench. An adjustable one. I walked right up to the kid behind the counter—young kid, couldn't have been more than nineteen, had his hat on backwards—and I told him exactly what I needed. He looked at me like I was speaking Greek. Kids today, they don't know the first thing about tools. When I was his age, I could take apart a lawnmower engine and put it back together blindfolded. My dad taught me. He had this massive workshop in the garage, pegboards lining every wall with an outline painted for every single tool so you always knew where it went. If you didn't put a hammer back in its exact outline, oh boy, you'd hear about it. He had this one ball-peen hammer that was his favorite, the handle was all worn smooth from years of use. He used it when he built our back deck. That deck stood for thirty years, solid as a rock. We used to have barbecues out there every Fourth of July. The whole neighborhood would come over, and my mom would make her famous potato salad. She put a little bit of mustard in it, gave it this nice tang...\n\nHold on, what were we talking about? Words? Right, right. No, I don't have any trouble finding words. I just say what I mean and mean what I say.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to things for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Paying attention for a long time? Well, yeah, I guess I do have some trouble with that. Like, I'll sit down at the bridge table or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings, you know? And I go in meaning to pay close attention, really focus on what's going on. But after a little while, my mind just sort of... slides off. Before I know it, I realize I missed half of what was said. It's like that time we were trying to organize the community garage sale. They were talking about the permit fees, and the next thing I know, I'm thinking about how much I used to love going to swap meets out in the desert. My buddy Frank and I used to drive out there in his old Chevy van. We'd find the craziest things. I bought this antique brass compass once, heavy thing, probably from an old ship. Frank bought a box of old license plates and nailed them all up in his garage. He had plates from all fifty states, even Alaska and Hawaii. The Hawaii one had a rainbow on it, I remember that clearly. He always said he wanted to visit Hawaii, go to a luau and eat a whole roasted pig...\n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, paying attention. Yeah, I try to listen, but my mind just wanders off and I miss things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Planning and organizing? No, I don't really think I have trouble with that. I mean, I don't really have to plan much these days anyway, being retired and all. I just sort of take things as they come. Like yesterday, I woke up and decided I was going to finally clean out the gutters. I got the ladder out of the shed—it's one of those heavy aluminum ones, extending kind. I bought it at a yard sale years ago from this guy who used to be a roofer. He had to quit because his knees gave out. Said he spent thirty years crawling around on hot asphalt shingles and it just ground the cartilage right down to nothing. He showed me the scars from his replacement surgeries, big ugly purple lines running right down the front of his legs. It made me glad I never did that kind of work. I spent most of my life working on the line at the auto plant. Putting on door panels, day in and day out. You'd think it would get boring, but there was a real rhythm to it, you know? The line never stopped moving, so you had to keep up. We had a good crew, though. My buddy Stan worked right next to me. Stan was a character, always chewing on a toothpick. He claimed it helped him quit smoking, but I swear he went through a whole box of toothpicks a week. He'd snap them in half with his teeth when he got stressed out about the quota...

Anyway, what was the question? Planning things? Yeah, no, I just don't have a lot on my plate to organize, so it's not really an issue.

No, no, I always know where I am. I might forget why I walked into the kitchen, but I know it's my kitchen. Which, by the way, the linoleum in there is starting to peel up by the sink. This month is a '67 Chevy Impala. Just a beautiful machine. I had a buddy back in high school, Dave, who had an Impala. We used to drive that thing all over town on the weekends. We'd go down to the old diner that used to be on Main Street—it's a bank now, which is a real shame because they made the absolute best cherry pie you've ever had in your life. The crust was always perfectly flaky, never soggy. The waitress there, Betty, she knew our orders by heart. She had this big blonde hair, you know, like they wore back then... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh! The time and the date. Right. No, I wear my trusty Timex every single day, so I always know exactly what time it is. I check the paper every morning, too. So I never really get confused about what day it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a bit of a talker, to be honest. My wife used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. She actually had this little collection of brass animals she kept on the mantelpiece. An elephant, a giraffe, and, well, a monkey. I bought her the elephant when we went to a flea market down in Florida years ago. We drove all the way down there in an '84 Winnebago. \n\nMan, that thing got terrible gas mileage, just absolutely drank it up. I think we spent half the trip just looking for gas stations. But gas was cheaper back then, you know? Not like today where you need a small loan just to fill up your tank. I went down to the corner station on Tuesday and nearly fell over looking at the sign. And the kid working the register, he couldn't have been more than sixteen, just had his face buried in his phone the whole time I was paying. Didn't even look up to say thank you. Kids these days, they just don't have the same manners we were taught. My father would have tanned my hide if I ignored an adult like that. He ran a little bait and tackle shop on the lake, and customer service was his gospel... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, right, finding the right words. No, no trouble with that at all. If anything, I've got too many of 'em!\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task or activity?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I'd say that's a bit of a struggle for me. Like, I can sit down at the bridge table with the guys, or go to one of those Fix-It Friends planning meetings—we meet up to do small repairs for the elderly in the neighborhood, mostly just changing lightbulbs or fixing leaky faucets. Last week I fixed Mrs. Gable's sink, the one over on Elm Street. She made me a plate of snickerdoodles for my trouble. Best cookies I've had since my wife passed, I tell you. She uses a little extra cinnamon, I think. Or maybe it's nutmeg? I remember my mother used to put nutmeg in her apple pies. She grew the apples right in our backyard. We had a big old Macintosh tree that produced so much fruit we'd be giving it away to half the town by September... \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'll sit down meaning to pay close attention to the game or the plan for the day, but after a little while, my mind just slides right off. Next thing I know, I realize I've missed half of what was said. It's like my brain just takes a little vacation without my permission.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself less interested in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like doing the things I always liked doing. I still go down to the community center on Thursdays for the woodworking group. We're building birdhouses right now. Well, most of the guys are building birdhouses. I decided to try my hand at a bat box. You know, bats are incredible creatures. They eat thousands of mosquitoes every single night. If you put up a bat box, you won't even need bug spray in the summer. I hate bug spray. It always leaves that sticky film on your skin, and the smell just gets right up into your nose and stays there for hours. I bought a can of that all-natural stuff once, citronella or something, but it didn't do a lick of good. The mosquitoes down by the lake just laughed at it. We used to go fishing at the lake every Sunday morning. Me and my brother, Tommy. We'd get out there before the sun was even fully up, when the water was still like glass. Tommy always caught more than me, but he had this lucky lure. A little silver spoon with a red feather on it. He swore by that thing. He lost it in the weeds one year and actually waded in up to his chest to get it back. Ruined his good boots... \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh! Enjoying things. Right, right.
